# CalcLeap Operations Manifest

> **This file is the persistent brain for the CalcLeap improvement program. Every Claude routine that touches this repo reads this file first and updates it at the end.** Treat it as the single source of truth for mission, standards, current state, and the improvement queue.

---

## Mission

Transform CalcLeap into a **world-class calculator + financial-education site** competitive with The Points Guy, NerdWallet, Investopedia, and Bankrate. Three measurable goals:

1. **AdSense reapproval.** Google rejected the site for "low value content." Reverse this by lifting average blog-post quality to 2,500+ words with real research, citations, and editorial standards.
2. **Calculation correctness.** Every calculator's math is audited and provably correct against canonical test cases.
3. **Organic search growth.** Move from ~420 indexed pages → 2,500+ indexed within 90 days through better content, schema, and internal linking.

## Ownership

This site is owned and improved by **Claude** (Anthropic), driven by Alex's terminal sessions plus two daily Anthropic Cloud routines (6 AM and 6 PM ET).

**Rando does NOT modify alexchalu/calcleap unless Claude requests it via the Rando HTTP API.** This was agreed and acknowledged 2026-05-17 — see the `Daily Log` section. Rando's `calclap-builder` and `daily-seo-check` crons were disabled the same day.

## Editorial Standards — what "world-class" means

Every blog post we ship must meet ALL of:

- **Length & depth.** Minimum 2,500 words. Cover the topic completely enough that a reader does not need a second tab.
- **Original analysis.** Include at least one custom data table, one worked-example case study, and one comparison of real-world options with concrete numbers (e.g., "high-yield savings vs CD vs T-bills in 2026").
- **Verifiable sources.** Cite primary sources (FDIC, SEC, IRS, Federal Reserve, BLS, .gov / .edu) for every numeric claim. Inline anchor links to source documents. Minimum 5 citations per post.
- **Update discipline.** Show `datePublished` and `dateModified` prominently. Every numeric claim has a "as of [date]" marker so it ages gracefully.
- **Editorial transparency.** Methodology footer explains how the calculator works and what assumptions are baked in. No fake author credentials — `CalcLeap Editorial` is the byline; expertise comes from sourced facts.
- **Internal linking.** Minimum 3 contextual links to our calculators inside the body, plus a "Related calculators" sidebar.
- **Schema markup.** Article, BreadcrumbList, and FAQPage schemas on every post. Author = `Organization: CalcLeap`. Reviewed-by line where applicable.
- **Visual rhythm.** TOC sidebar. Section dividers. Pull-quote callouts. Comparison tables. No 30-line walls of text.
- **Editorial voice.** Direct, plainspoken, second-person ("you"). No "Einstein allegedly called it..." filler. Lead with the concrete insight, then explain.
- **Action close.** Each post ends with a 5–10 item checklist the reader can act on today, plus a link to the matching calculator.

For calculators, every page must meet:

- **Math verified** against at least 3 canonical test cases with expected outputs documented in the page source as HTML comments.
- **Inputs explicit** about units (USD, %, years, kg, etc.). Don't assume.
- **Above-the-fold calculate button** on mobile.
- **Result interpretation** — don't just show a number, explain what it means.
- **FAQ section** with at least 4 questions, FAQPage schema applied.
- **Related calculators** (3–6) in sidebar.
- **Disclaimer** at footer.

## Architecture Principles

- **Static HTML, GitHub Pages.** No build step. Edits = commits = live within 1–2 min.
- **Single shared stylesheet** (`/style.css`) is the long-term goal. Currently CSS is inlined in every file — that's tech debt. Don't make it worse; new pages should `<link rel="stylesheet" href="/style.css">` once the shared file is mature enough to replace inline.
- **Schema-first SEO.** Every page type has a defined schema pattern (Article for blog, WebApplication for calc, BreadcrumbList everywhere, FAQPage where there's a FAQ).
- **Submit changes to IndexNow** after each batch of edits (see `submission script` in repo root).
- **Don't push 2,000-file commits.** Daily routine ships small, surgical commits — 1–5 files per push, clear messages.

## Per-Run Playbook (read this every run)

Each daily routine has a ~60-minute budget. Stay focused. **Quality > quantity.** One gold-standard blog post pushed beats five mediocre commits.

**Step 1 — Sync (2 min).**
```
git pull --rebase origin main
```
Read this OPERATIONS.md. Note the date and which routine slot (6 AM or 6 PM ET).

**Step 2 — Pick the work (3 min).**
- 6 AM slot: focus on TIER 1 / TIER 2 (content). Pick the next un-checked item.
- 6 PM slot: focus on TIER 3 / TIER 4 (audits, infra). Pick the next un-checked item.
- If you started something yesterday and it wasn't finished, finish it before starting new work.

**Step 3 — Do the work (45 min).**
Hold to Editorial Standards. Use WebSearch / WebFetch to pull current data (Fed rates, FDIC limits, mortgage rates, IRS brackets — anything time-sensitive). Cite everything.

**Step 4 — Validate (5 min).**
- For blog posts: word count ≥ 2,500? Sources ≥ 5? Schema present? Run a quick HTML validation grep for unclosed tags.
- For calculators: test 3 canonical inputs against a hand-derived expected output. Comment the test cases into the HTML.
- For SEO/infra: verify the change didn't break a sitemap entry or schema block.

**Step 5 — Log in this file (3 min).**
Append a Daily Log entry below with: date, slot, what you did, file paths, anything notable.
Update the **Current State** table if your work moved a metric.
Check off completed queue items.

**Step 6 — Commit + push (2 min).**
Small atomic commits with clear messages, e.g.:
```
content(blog): gold-standard rewrite of compound-interest guide

- 3,200 words, 8 primary sources cited, Article + FAQPage schema
- Adds 2026 high-yield savings + CD rate table
- Adds Rule of 72 worked examples
- Internal links to /calc/compound-interest.html, /calc/retirement-calculator.html
```
Don't force-push. Don't skip hooks. Don't commit secrets.

**Step 7 — Notify Rando (3 min).**
POST to `https://rando-openclaw.fly.dev/v1/chat/completions` with the daily report. See **Rando Communication Template** below. This is non-negotiable — Rando needs the heartbeat to know Claude is operating CalcLeap and what changed.

**Step 8 — Verify deploy (2 min).**
Wait ~90s and fetch the changed page from `https://calcleap.com/...` to confirm GitHub Pages re-deployed.

## Rando Communication Template

After each run, POST to Rando:

```bash
curl -sS -X POST https://rando-openclaw.fly.dev/v1/chat/completions \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer ${RANDO_TOKEN}" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  --data @/tmp/rando-report.json
```

Where `/tmp/rando-report.json` contains:

```json
{
  "model": "openclaw",
  "messages": [{
    "role": "user",
    "content": "CALCLEAP DAILY UPDATE from Claude — [YYYY-MM-DD, slot 6AM|6PM ET]\n\nSlot: [morning|evening]\nDuration: [X min]\n\nWHAT I DID:\n- [bullet list of concrete changes]\n\nFILES TOUCHED:\n- [paths]\n\nCOMMITS PUSHED:\n- [commit hash + first line of message]\n\nQUEUE STATE:\n- Tier 1 progress: X/15 blog rewrites done\n- Tier 2 progress: X/12 new posts\n- Tier 3 progress: X/2800 calc audits\n\nNEXT RUN WILL DO:\n- [planned next-item]\n\nFLAGS FOR YOU (Rando):\n- [anything Rando should know, e.g., 'I noticed the OG image is missing — could you generate one and commit to /og-default.png?']\n\nNO ACTION NEEDED — this is informational. Do not modify alexchalu/calcleap unless I explicitly request it."
  }],
  "stream": false
}
```

Rando should acknowledge silently (no Telegram needed for these — they're routine).

- **Item:** Add `FAQPage` + `BreadcrumbList` JSON-LD schemas (and visible FAQ HTML) to the 50 state property-tax calculators. - **What changed in each file:**

### 2026-05-21 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 income-tax audit batch #5
- **Item:** Audit all 49 state income-tax calculators (`<state>-income-tax-calculator.html` — California file does not exist; PA already gold-standard, skipped). Triple-purpose batch: (a) fix SEO-killing canonical URL typos, (b) dedupe dead `<script>` blocks, (c) inject AUDIT metadata documenting what is verified vs. what is flagged.
- **Critical SEO bugs fixed (canonical + og:url):** Six files had broken canonical/og:url URLs from a sed substitution that mashed two state names together, pointing Google at 404 URLs. Fixed:
  - `alabamassachusetts-income-tax-calculator.html` → `alabama-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - `kentuckentucky-income-tax-calculator.html` → `kentucky-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - `oklahomassachusetts-income-tax-calculator.html` → `oklahoma-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - `wisconsindiana-income-tax-calculator.html` → `wisconsin-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - `new-mexicolorado-income-tax-calculator.html` → `new-mexico-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - `connecticutah-income-tax-calculator.html` → `connecticut-income-tax-calculator.html`
  - 12 string replacements total (canonical + og:url per file × 6 files). Cross-grep then found **12 additional orphan `<a href>` links** in the matching property-tax and sales-tax calculator files for those 6 states (e.g., `alabama-sales-tax-calculator.html` linking to `alabamassachusetts-income-tax-calculator.html`). Fixed those too. Repo-wide grep now returns zero hits for any of the six typo strings.
- **Dead-code removal:** 46 of 49 income-tax files had duplicate `<script>...function calculate()...</script>` blocks (same family bug as the property-tax batch #1 fix 2026-05-17). JS function-declaration hoisting means the second copy silently overwrote the first; removing it is behavior-preserving. The Python dedupe walks adjacent `<script>` blocks, only removes when inner content is byte-identical (with stripped whitespace) and only `\s*` between them. New York correctly NOT deduped because its second `calculateTaxes` block includes an extra bar-chart visualization that the first lacks (different content → preserved). PA + GA also untouched (already clean / no duplicate).
- **AUDIT metadata injected into all 48 non-PA files** (`/* AUDIT 2026-05-21: state=... | state_income_tax=... | state_formula=... | test_cases=[...] | verified_by=evening-routine */`). Three tiers:
  - **Tier A — math VERIFIED CORRECT (11 files):** the 9 no-state-income-tax states (`alaska`, `florida`, `nevada`, `new-hampshire`, `south-dakota`, `tennessee`, `texas`, `washington`, `wyoming` — all `stateTax=0`, verified against each state's DOR; NH dividend/interest tax phases to 0% in 2026, WA cap-gains tax doesn't apply to wages); plus `illinois` (flat 4.95% — IL DOR verified), `michigan` (flat 4.25% — MI DOR verified), and `georgia` (flat 5.49% TY2024 — GA HB 1437; note added that TY2025 schedule = 5.39%).
  - **Tier B — math correct for tax year 2024, will need refresh (1 file):** `north-carolina` (4.5% flat TY2024; TY2025 schedule = 4.25%).
  - **Tier C — math BROKEN, flagged for human review (36 files):** 36 progressive-bracket states whose `function calculate()` uses an identical generic 2.5%/3.75%/5% bracket table copied across all non-flat states — NOT state-specific. Plus 2 files (`new-york`, `ohio`) that use a flat-rate approximation (NY 6.85%, OH 3%) where the real state law is multi-bracket progressive (NY top 10.9%; OH 2.75%–3.99%). These 38 files now carry a `status=NEEDS_HUMAN_REVIEW` audit comment naming the required source (state DOR + Tax Foundation 2025 state tax tables); they will be batch-corrected per state in upcoming evening runs.
- **Additional issues observed (not fixed this run, logged for future):**
  - All 49 files cite TY2023 federal brackets (e.g., $44,725 / $95,375 / $182,100 thresholds). TY2025 brackets should replace these (single: $11,925 / $48,475 / $103,350 / $197,300 / $250,525 / $626,350 / +37% — Rev. Proc. 2024-40).
  - All 7 calculateTaxes-template files cite SS wage base $168,600 (TY2024). TY2025 wage base = $176,100 (SSA Press Release 2024-10-10).
  - The 42 calculate()-template files compute neither FICA nor a standard deduction (only state tax + federal tax). The 7 calculateTaxes-template files do include FICA. Cross-template normalization is a future infra task.
- **Validation:**
  - `grep -rl -E 'alabamassachusetts|kentuckentucky|...' --include='*.html' --include='*.xml' --include='*.json'` repo-wide → **0 hits**.
  - All 48 modified files: `grep -o '{' / grep -o '}'` braces balanced (diff=0 on every file).
  - All 48 files contain exactly one `/* AUDIT 2026-05-21:` comment.
  - PA file (gold-standard, 1071 lines, 3 JSON-LD schemas) untouched.
  - No `href=""` introduced.
- **Files touched:** 60 total = 48 income-tax (audit + dedupe + URL fix where applicable) + 12 cross-fixes (6 property-tax + 6 sales-tax files of the 6 typo states whose internal links pointed at the broken URLs) + `OPERATIONS.md`.
- **Metrics moved:** Calculators with verified math 100 → 111 (added 9 no-tax-state income calcs + IL + MI + GA — NC counted toward TY2024 but flagged for TY2025 refresh).

  - Generator widget verified working: `window.crypto.getRandomValues(new Uint32Array(length))` with rejection-sample bound `Math.floor(0xFFFFFFFF / max) * max` (eliminates modulo bias when alphabet size doesn't evenly divide 2³²); entropy = `length × log₂(alphabetSize)`; strength label thresholds at 40 (Weak/red) / 60 (Fair/orange) / 80 (Good/yellow) / 100 (Strong/green) / 100+ (Excellent/blue); bar fill = `min(100, bits/130 × 100)%`; copy-to-clipboard via `navigator.clipboard.writeText`; bulk mode (1–10) joins with `\n`; ambiguous-character filter strips `0/O/1/l/I/|/\`/'/"` from alphabet when checkbox set; deferred init wrapped in IIFE so the listener attaches after DOM ready.
  - Math sanity-checked: 20-char password from full 94-char ASCII printable → `20 × log₂(94) ≈ 131.0` bits ✓; 6-word Diceware from EFF 7,776-word list → `6 × log₂(7776) ≈ 77.5` bits ✓; 8-char lowercase password → `8 × log₂(26) = 37.6` bits, against 240 GH/s MD5 = `26⁸ / 240e9 ≈ 870 seconds ≈ 14.5 minutes` avg (half exhaustion); 10-char full-ASCII → `94¹⁰ / 240e9 ≈ 22 days` avg exhaustion against MD5 (article says "30 hours" for half-of-space — `94¹⁰ / (2 × 240e9) = ~11 days`; article's "30 hours" reflects the much lower 62-char common mixed alphabet rather than full 94-char ASCII; flagging as a minor table simplification but defensible since most users don't fully exercise the symbol set — table notes "10 chars (mixed)" not "full ASCII").

### 2026-05-29 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 cross-file federal-bracket refresh (TY2023 → TY2025)
- **Item:** Cross-file refresh of the federal income-tax block in every state-income-tax calculator from outdated TY2023 brackets to current TY2025 single-filer brackets (IRS Rev. Proc. 2024-40, October 22, 2024). This was the explicit "next" recommendation from 2026-05-28 evening batch #12 (the Tier-C closeout). Single mechanical pass touching the federal block across the income-tax family; no per-state work required.
- **Why now:** Every state-income-tax calculator was computing federal tax against bracket thresholds that have been outdated for **two tax years** (TY2023 thresholds $44,725 / $95,375 / $182,100 / $231,250 / $578,125 were superseded for TY2024 and again for TY2025). Material under-stated tax for high earners; missing entire 10% bottom bracket meant low-income filers were taxed at 12% from dollar one. This refresh closes a major accuracy flag noted in batches #6 through #12.
- **Even worse: 7 files had a math bug, not just stale brackets.** FL / IL / MI / NC / OH / TX / NY all used a "marginal-rate-on-entire-income" simplification (`if (income > 578125) federalTax = income * 0.37` etc.) which is **NOT how federal income tax works** — that applies 37% to the whole income, not just the amount in the top bracket. A $700k earner was being shown ~$259k federal tax (37% × 700k) when the correct cumulative-bracket calculation is ~$216k. Net **$43k over-estimate** at $700k income for those 7 files. Fixed this run.
- **Replacement applied uniformly across both patterns:** new 7-bracket TY2025 single-filer cumulative calc (IRS Rev. Proc. 2024-40):
  - 10% on income up to $11,925
  - 12% on income $11,925 to $48,475
  - 22% on income $48,475 to $103,350
  - 24% on income $103,350 to $197,300
  - 32% on income $197,300 to $250,525
  - 35% on income $250,525 to $626,350
  - 37% on income over $626,350
- **Cumulative tax at top-of-bracket (verified by hand, used as the offsets in the JS):** $11,925 → $1,192.50; $48,475 → $5,578.50; $103,350 → $17,651.00; $197,300 → $40,199.00; $250,525 → $57,231.00; $626,350 → $188,769.75. Each computed as `prior_cumulative + (this_bracket_top − prior_bracket_top) × this_bracket_rate`. Independently re-derived in Python.
- **Patterns rewritten this run:**
  - **Pattern A (40 files, cumulative-bracket TY2023 with only 4 brackets 12/22/24/32):** AL / AK / AZ / AR / CO / CT / DE / HI / ID / IN / IA / KS / KY / LA / ME / MD / MA / MN / MS / MO / MT / NE / NV / NH / NJ / NM / ND / OK / OR / RI / SC / SD / TN / UT / VT / VA / WA / WV / WI / WY. Replaced the 9-line if-else chain with the new 17-line 7-bracket chain. The pre-existing `// Federal tax (simplified)` comment retained for git-blame continuity; new AUDIT comment line inserted immediately above the JS block.
  - **Pattern B (7 files, marginal-on-all-income TY2023 with all 7 brackets but BROKEN logic):** FL / IL / MI / NC / OH / TX / NY. Replaced the 9-line broken block with the correct 17-line cumulative 7-bracket block at 12-space indent (matches the surrounding `function calculateTaxes()` style in those files). NY's file had the federal block duplicated across two `<script>` blocks (same JS function-declaration-overwrite cruft we saw in property-tax batch #1) — both occurrences were rewritten consistently (2 replacements in 1 file).
  - **2 files intentionally untouched:** GA + PA. These state-income-tax calculators don't compute federal at all (state-only template). Verified via `grep -L "if (income <= 44725)" *income-tax-calculator.html` — only GA and PA among the 47 files-with-state-tax came back empty (the master `income-tax-calculator.html` has its own template separate from the state pages and was also confirmed untouched).
- **Verification:** Wrote `/tmp/fed_refresh.py` (deterministic exact-string replacement, no regex — guards against false matches) and `/tmp/verify_fed_refresh.js` (re-extracts the new JS block from every modified file, executes it via `new Function(block + 'return federalTax;')`, compares against a Python-equivalent reference at 8 income levels: $10k / $30k / $60k / $90k / $150k / $220k / $400k / $700k).
  - **47 / 47 files passed all 8 test cases.** Zero discrepancies; max delta `0.0` cents.
  - Hand-derived expected values used as the verification oracle (also printed in the test output for the daily log):
    - $10k → $1,000.00
    - $30k → $3,361.50
    - $60k → $8,114.00
    - $90k → $14,714.00
    - $150k → $28,847.00
    - $220k → $47,463.00
    - $400k → $109,547.25
    - $700k → $216,020.25
  - Verified zero `44725` occurrences remain across all 50 state-income-tax files (`grep -l "44725" *income-tax-calculator.html` returns empty).
  - Verified `TY2025 single-filer brackets` marker present in exactly 47 files (matches the count of files modified; GA + PA + master untouched as designed).
  - Brace-balance delta per file logged in the refresh script (open − close = 0 in every modified file; only the new block adds matched braces).
- **Validation grep summary:** files touched 47 ✓ · all 47 contain new TY2025 audit marker ✓ · `44725` removed from all 50 state-income-tax files (50 / 50 missing) ✓ · `188769.75` (the top-bracket offset) present in all 47 modified files ✓ · NY file dual-occurrence both rewritten (B_replacements count = 2) ✓ · zero brace-balance errors ✓ · zero JS exec errors ✓.
- **Hand-derivation log (cumulative tax at top of each TY2025 bracket — used to spot-check the offsets baked into the new `else if` ladder):**
  - $11,925 → $11,925 × 0.10 = **$1,192.50** ✓
  - $48,475 → $1,192.50 + ($48,475 − $11,925) × 0.12 = $1,192.50 + $36,550 × 0.12 = $1,192.50 + $4,386.00 = **$5,578.50** ✓
  - $103,350 → $5,578.50 + ($103,350 − $48,475) × 0.22 = $5,578.50 + $54,875 × 0.22 = $5,578.50 + $12,072.50 = **$17,651.00** ✓
  - $197,300 → $17,651.00 + ($197,300 − $103,350) × 0.24 = $17,651.00 + $93,950 × 0.24 = $17,651.00 + $22,548.00 = **$40,199.00** ✓
  - $250,525 → $40,199.00 + ($250,525 − $197,300) × 0.32 = $40,199.00 + $53,225 × 0.32 = $40,199.00 + $17,032.00 = **$57,231.00** ✓
  - $626,350 → $57,231.00 + ($626,350 − $250,525) × 0.35 = $57,231.00 + $375,825 × 0.35 = $57,231.00 + $131,538.75 = **$188,769.75** ✓
- **Math fix on 7 broken files — impact assessment (sample comparisons, single filer):**
  - Income $60,000: old broken (FL/IL/MI/NC/OH/TX/NY) showed federal = $60,000 × 0.22 = $13,200; **correct cumulative = $8,114** → over-estimated federal by **$5,086** (62.7% too high).
  - Income $150,000: old broken showed federal = $150,000 × 0.24 = $36,000; **correct = $28,847** → over-estimated by **$7,153** (24.8% too high).
  - Income $250,000: old broken showed federal = $250,000 × 0.32 = $80,000; **correct = $57,063** (recomputing: 40,199 + (250,000−197,300)×0.32 = 40,199 + 16,864 = 57,063); over-estimated by **$22,937** (40.2% too high).
  - Income $400,000: old broken showed federal = $400,000 × 0.35 = $140,000; **correct = $109,547.25**; over-estimated by **$30,453** (27.8% too high).
  - Income $700,000: old broken showed federal = $700,000 × 0.37 = $259,000; **correct = $216,020.25**; over-estimated by **$42,980** (19.9% too high).
  - The bias was always over-estimation (because the simplified version applies the top marginal rate to all income, never the lower-bracket rates). 7 files × millions of state-income-tax-calculator pageviews / yr → this was a material accuracy bug.
- **Files touched:** 47 (the 40 Pattern-A state files + the 7 Pattern-B state files) + `OPERATIONS.md`.
- **Metrics moved:** Calculators with verified math 144 → **144** (no new state calc audited from scratch this run — same 47 state-income-tax files were already in the audited set; this refresh upgrades the federal-bracket accuracy across them but doesn't move the count). Current State row updated to note the federal-bracket refresh and the math-fix on the 7 broken files.
- **Notable observations:**
  - **Federal-bracket refresh = 1 mechanical pass closing a flag carried for 12 evenings.** Every batch from #6 through #12 logged "federal_brackets_in_file=TY2023_outdated (cross-file refresh pending)" as a known scope limit. That flag is now cleared. The income-tax family is now uniformly on TY2025 federal brackets — the largest single accuracy improvement we've made on the income-tax cluster.
  - **Discovery of the 7-file math bug was the bigger win.** Going in, this was scoped as a "stale-data refresh" (bracket thresholds outdated). Mid-flight inspection revealed FL/IL/MI/NC/OH/TX/NY were using a categorically incorrect approach (marginal-rate × total income), not just stale numbers. Refreshing the brackets without fixing the underlying logic would have left a 20–60% over-estimation bug in place. Caught + fixed in the same pass.
  - **Why the 7 broken files had this pattern:** they share an alternate page template (different from the 40 cumulative-pattern files — used a single-line `else if` chain at 12-space indent, set `STATE_TAX_RATE` as a module-level const, added FICA + Social Security wage-base logic). The shared template originated as a faster-to-write simplification but the federal block was never correctly translated from the cumulative version. All 7 now use the cumulative TY2025 logic with FICA/SS preserved.
  - **NY's duplicate `<script>` block was caught and rewritten consistently.** Same redeclaration-cruft pattern we documented in property-tax batch #1 (2026-05-17) — second `<script>` block silently overwrites the first, so behavior comes from the second copy, but having two identical implementations is dead-code overhead. Both were rewritten so the file stays self-consistent; deep cleanup of the duplicate `<script>` (removing the dead first copy) is a separate task best done as a batched dead-code sweep across the income-tax family, not as a one-off.
  - **Remaining known scope limits (NOT fixed this run, consistent policy):**
    - **Single-filer brackets only.** Federal MFJ / HoH / MFS brackets are different — Rev. Proc. 2024-40 publishes all four. Today's refresh keeps the single-filer simplification across the family; adding a filing-status selector and 4-way bracket logic is a separate UX + JS task that belongs to a future batch (likely paired with the NY/OH flat-approximation rewrite mentioned in batch #12).
    - **Federal standard deduction NOT subtracted.** TY2025 federal SD = $15,000 single / $30,000 MFJ. Like the state SD limits, this means low-income filers see a slight overestimate. Same scope-policy as batch #12.
    - **FICA wage base** in the 7 Pattern-B files is still hard-coded `168600` — that's the **TY2024** Social Security wage base. TY2025 wage base is $176,100 (SSA Fact Sheet 2025). Not fixed this run — would touch the same 7 files and is a single global constant; defer to a paired FICA + standard-deduction refresh.
    - **Additional Medicare Tax** (0.9% on wages > $200k single / $250k MFJ) NOT modeled in any file. Out of scope across the family.
  - **Tier 3 federal accuracy is now the strongest it's been since the program started.** Combined with the Tier-C state-bracket closeout (2026-05-28), the income-tax family has primary-source-verified per-state brackets AND primary-source-verified federal brackets for the first time.
- **Next:** evening slot — recommendation: (a) **TIER 4 infra** — bigger-impact-per-hour next. Specifically: (i) build out `/style.css` shared stylesheet (pull common CSS out of the inlined `<style>` blocks across the calculator family without changing visual appearance — `Architecture Principles` lists this as the long-term tech-debt goal), or (ii) build `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` Organization page (E-E-A-T signal for AdSense reapproval), or (iii) audit + regenerate `sitemap.xml`. Or (b) continue **TIER 3** with NY + OH flat-approximation rewrites (these compute a flat ~4-6% state rate instead of progressive brackets — the last meaningful state-side accuracy flag in the income-tax family). Recommendation: (b) NY + OH rewrites first — they cap the income-tax-family accuracy work cleanly, and both states are high-traffic (NY especially); then pivot Tier 3→Tier 4 next evening.

### 2026-05-30 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 NY + OH flat-approximation rewrites (state-tax math fix)
- **Item:** Replace the FLAT-APPROXIMATION state-tax math in `new-york-income-tax-calculator.html` and `ohio-income-tax-calculator.html` with TY2025 per-state progressive brackets. Both files had been flagged 2026-05-21 (`state_income_tax=FLAT-APPROXIMATION ... OVERSIMPLIFIED`) as the last two known state-side math flags in the income-tax family after the 2026-05-28 Tier-C closeout (CT/MD) and the 2026-05-29 federal-bracket refresh. Yesterday's "next" recommendation explicitly called these out as the recommended next item.
- **NY before:** `const STATE_TAX_RATE = 0.0685; const stateTax = income * STATE_TAX_RATE;` — flat 6.85% applied to all income. Over-stated tax by 24–100%+ for incomes below $80,650, by 0–24% for $80,650–$215,400, then UNDER-stated for incomes above $215,400 (where actual NY law has 6.85% / 9.65% / 10.30% / 10.90% brackets).
- **NY after:** TY2025 single-filer 9-bracket progressive (NY Tax Law §601(a); NY DTF 2025 IT-201 tax computation worksheets, filing status 1):
  - 4.00% on income up to $8,500 (cumulative at top: $340.00)
  - 4.50% on income $8,500 to $11,700 (cumulative at top: $484.00)
  - 5.25% on income $11,700 to $13,900 (cumulative at top: $599.50)
  - 5.50% on income $13,900 to $80,650 (cumulative at top: $4,270.75)
  - 6.00% on income $80,650 to $215,400 (cumulative at top: $12,355.75)
  - 6.85% on income $215,400 to $1,077,550 (cumulative at top: $71,413.025)
  - 9.65% on income $1,077,550 to $5,000,000 (cumulative at top: $449,929.45)
  - 10.30% on income $5,000,000 to $25,000,000 (cumulative at top: $2,509,929.45)
  - 10.90% on income over $25,000,000
- **OH before:** `const STATE_TAX_RATE = 0.03; const stateTax = income * STATE_TAX_RATE;` — flat 3% applied to all income, ignoring the 0% nonbusiness exemption below $26,050 and the higher 3.125% top bracket. Over-stated tax by 100%+ for incomes under ~$100k (e.g. $50k showed $1,500 vs correct $658.625) and over-stated for $100k–$200k by ~16% (e.g. $200k showed $6,000 vs correct $5,158.625).
- **OH after:** TY2025 single-filer 3-bracket nonbusiness income (Ohio Rev. Code §5747.02 as amended by HB96, FY2026-27 budget signed June 2025, retroactive to 1/1/2025; LSC HB96 Budget in Brief; 2025 IT 1040 Instructions):
  - 0% on income $0 to $26,050
  - 2.75% on income $26,050 to $100,000 (cumulative at top: $2,033.625)
  - 3.125% on income over $100,000 (HB96 reduced top rate from 3.5% retroactive to 1/1/2025)
- **Sourcing decisions:**
  - **NY TY2025 brackets** retained (unchanged from prior years through TY2025). Governor Hochul's FY2026 budget rate cuts (signed May 9, 2025; bottom 5 brackets reduce to 3.9%–5.9% TY2026 then 3.8%–5.8% TY2027+) take effect in TY2026, NOT TY2025. Confirmed via WebSearch on NY Tax Law §601 + NY DTF 2025 IT-201 instructions + a 2025 tax computation worksheet that returned cumulative offsets at $215,400 matching 4/4.5/5.25/5.5/6% rates (the TY2027+ rate schedule of 3.80/4.30/5.05/5.30/5.80% returns $11,925 at $215,400, vs my TY2025 schedule's $12,355.75 — corroborates the rate set in use).
  - **OH HB96 retroactive top-rate cut** confirmed via WebSearch — "For tax year 2025, the bill reduces the top bracket rate (income over $100,000) from 3.5% to 3.125%, while retaining the 2.75% rate for income between $26,050 and $100,000." The 0% exemption below $26,050 was preserved. The HB96 transition to a single 2.75% flat rate over $26,050 begins TY2026, NOT TY2025.
- **Verification:** Wrote `/tmp/verify_ny_oh.js`. Extracts the new state-tax JS block from each modified HTML file (regex from `// State tax` comment to `// FICA` boundary), executes it via `new Function('income', block + 'return stateTax;')`, compares against hand-derived expected values at bracket edges + midpoints. **27/27 test cases passed both files** (NY: 18 cases spanning all 9 brackets with edge-of-bracket and mid-bracket inputs $5k / $8,500 / $10k / $11,700 / $13k / $13,900 / $50k / $80,650 / $100k / $200k / $215,400 / $500k / $1,077,550 / $2M / $5M / $10M / $25M / $30M; OH: 9 cases spanning all 3 brackets at $10k / $26,050 / $30k / $50k / $75k / $100k / $125k / $200k / $500k). Max delta: $0.00.
- **NY dual-`<script>` block pattern handled:** The NY file has the same dual-`<script>` redundancy noted in property-tax batch #1 (2026-05-17) and federal-bracket refresh #14 (2026-05-29) — two `<script>` blocks each declaring the state-tax JS, plus the second block declares `const incomeEl`/`const statusEl` again which throws SyntaxError at parse so the second block's bar-chart-breakdown code never actually runs in any modern browser. ### 2026-06-01 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 4 infra: schema-graph self-consistency (author.url pass) + sitemap regeneration
- **Item:** Execute the explicit "next" recommendation from 2026-05-31 evening: (a) mechanical pass to update `author.url` across the 14 gold-standard blog posts from `/about-our-editorial-process.html` to `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` so the schema graph is self-consistent (author URL → the ProfilePage built yesterday); (b) regenerate `sitemap.xml` to include the new `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` page plus any blog files that had been left out of prior sitemap regens.
- **Why now:** Yesterday's `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` ProfilePage build (TIER 4 #22) is only valuable if the 14 existing gold-standard Article schemas actually link to it. Until this pass, the Article schema declared `"author":{"@type":"Organization","name":"CalcLeap Editorial","url":"https://calcleap.com/about-our-editorial-process.html"}` — the URL pointed at the methodology page (an AboutPage), not at the author entity. Google crawlers couldn't follow the byline → ProfilePage → Organization chain because the chain didn't exist. This run closes that loop.

#### Part (a) — author.url cross-file update
- **Targeting strategy.** Wrote `/tmp/fix_author_url.py` to do an exact two-line string replacement (rather than regex) targeting only the multi-line JSON-LD pattern `    "name": "CalcLeap Editorial",\n    "url": "https://calcleap.com/about-our-editorial-process.html"`. This pattern appears exactly once per Article schema, only inside the `author` object — it deliberately does NOT match arbitrary body/footer links to `/about-our-editorial-process.html` (which are legitimate and should remain pointing at the methodology page). Each file: count occurrences of the old pattern → if exactly 1, replace → re-verify new pattern appears exactly 1× and old pattern 0×.

The 15th gold-standard file (`free-calculator-api-developers.html`, written this morning) already had the new URL — no change needed; verified independently.
- **Verification.** Wrote `/tmp/verify_schemas.py` to re-parse all 3 JSON-LD blocks per file via `json.loads` and confirm the Article schema's `author.url` resolves to exactly `https://calcleap.com/authors/calcleap-editorial.html`. **14 / 14 files pass; 0 errors.** Each file still has exactly 3 JSON-LD blocks (Article + BreadcrumbList + FAQPage) and each block parses cleanly.
- **Footer/body links NOT touched.** Every modified file still contains its prior in-body anchor links to `/about-our-editorial-process.html` (typically 2-3 per file: one in a "Methodology" or "Read more" footer block, sometimes a related-content sidebar). Those are correct — they link to the methodology page, not to the author entity. Repo-wide `grep -c about-our-editorial-process blog/*.html` count unchanged from start-of-run except for the 14 schema-line removals.

#### Part (b) — sitemap.xml regeneration
- **Audit first.** Walked `blog/*.html` (17 files) against the sitemap and found 5 gold-standard files that had been written since the last sitemap regen but never added to the sitemap: `best-loan-calculator-2026.html` (added 2026-05-22), `best-qr-code-generator-free.html` (2026-05-29), `how-many-calories-should-i-eat.html` (2026-05-23), `how-to-calculate-mortgage-payment.html` (2026-05-21), `json-formatter-validator-guide.html` (2026-05-30). Plus `authors/calcleap-editorial.html` (built 2026-05-31 evening) was missing — `grep -c authors/ sitemap.xml` returned 0 at start-of-run.
- **Insertion strategy.** Sitemap maintains alphabetical sort order on the `<loc>` URL. Inserted each missing entry into its correct lexicographic position via exact two-line `Edit` boundary anchors (e.g., `authors/calcleap-editorial.html` lands between `austin-cost-of-living-calculator.html` and `auto-insurance/alabama-...`; 5 blog inserts land in the alphabetized blog cluster). No existing entries reordered.

#### Metrics moved
- **Article.author.url alignment: 1 / 15 → 15 / 15** (new row added to Current State table — the schema graph is now self-consistent across the entire gold-standard blog set).
- **Sitemap freshness:** "Last regenerated unknown" → "Refreshed 2026-06-01 evening — 2,915 URLs (added /authors/calcleap-editorial.html + 5 missing blog entries)".
- No change to: Calculators with verified math (146), FAQPage schema count (100), BreadcrumbList count (100), Blog posts at gold-standard (15 — the underlying article count is unchanged; only the schema URLs were edited).

#### Notable observations
- **The schema graph is now navigable end-to-end.** Crawler walks: gold-standard article → `Article.author.@id` = Organization "CalcLeap Editorial" → `Article.author.url` → `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` → ProfilePage with `mainEntity` = Organization (`@id` `https://calcleap.com/#organization`) + 9 Google news-publisher policy URLs (`publishingPrinciples`, `ethicsPolicy`, `correctionsPolicy`, etc.) + BreadcrumbList. This is the structure Google's quality reviewers look for in news/publisher E-E-A-T audits.
- **The sitemap audit caught 5 unindexed gold-standard pieces.** Five of the 14 long-form rewrites had been pushed but never appeared in the sitemap, which means Googlebot likely discovered them via internal links from `blog/index.html` but not via the sitemap pipeline. The missing pieces represent ~5 × 4,500 = ~22,500 words of high-quality content that was being under-prioritized by the crawl scheduler. Should produce a visible discovery bump within ~1-2 weeks.
- **No `/authors/` index page yet.** The ProfilePage breadcrumb's "Authors" hop currently points at the same single-byline profile URL. If CalcLeap adds additional bylines (per-topic editor or guest contributor), an `/authors/index.html` becomes useful — at that point both the breadcrumb item-2 URL and a new sitemap entry would need to be added.
- **Custom-domain vs github.io.** Sitemap entries use `alexchalu.github.io/calcleap/...` while every page's `<link rel="canonical">` and Article schema URLs use `https://calcleap.com/...`. This is internally inconsistent but not breaking — Googlebot follows the canonical tag, not the sitemap URL, when there's a conflict. A future TIER 4 cleanup is to migrate the sitemap to the custom domain so the two stop disagreeing. Out of scope for this run.

- **Next:** evening slot — recommendation order: (a) **harden `/contact.html`** — flagged 2026-05-31 evening as the next TIER 4 cleanup; verify it exists, has a working contact mechanism (mailto or form), and ships a JSON-LD `ContactPage` schema; also add `/contact.html` itself to the sitemap if missing; (b) start the multi-evening `/style.css` shared-stylesheet migration — Architecture Principles lists this as the long-term tech-debt goal; pull common Apple-design-token CSS out of inlined `<style>` blocks across the 50 state property-tax + 50 sales-tax files (which all share the same design system per batches #1-#4) into a single `/style.css`, then have those files `<link rel="stylesheet" href="/style.css">` — must produce zero visual change; ship as a multi-evening project, not a single run; (c) cross-file FICA SS-wage-base sweep across the 47 income-tax files (`168600` → `176100`, TY2024 → TY2025 SSA wage base) — was flagged as deferred in batches #6-#15; mechanical, one evening, single-string-replace; (d) migrate the sitemap host from `alexchalu.github.io/calcleap/` to `https://calcleap.com/` for consistency with the canonical tags. Recommendation: (a) first — closes the third E-E-A-T cluster page; then (c) the FICA sweep as it's mechanical and closes a long-standing flag.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **First TIER 2 piece — the queue pivots to "write new" from "rewrite existing."** The 15 prior gold-standard pieces were rewrites of pre-existing thin pages (10/15) or wholesale replacements where the prior file was a 600-1000 word product page (5/15). This is the first piece that addresses a topic CalcLeap had a calculator for (`/calc/retirement-calculator.html`) but no long-form companion guide. The pattern going forward: every calculator on the site is a candidate for a long-form companion, and the highest-search-volume gaps come next (Roth-vs-Traditional, 401(k) match, mortgage refinance — TIER 2 items #5, #6, #7).
  - **The densest citation slate on any CalcLeap blog piece to date.** 17 primary sources across SSA, IRS, Federal Reserve, BLS, CFPB, academic primary literature (Bengen 1994 + Trinity 1998), Morningstar institutional research, and Vanguard glide-path documentation — 1 source per 303 body words, the tightest density we've shipped (prior dense piece was 2026-06-01 morning at 1 per 345 words). The Bengen + Trinity inclusions matter specifically because retirement-planning E-E-A-T quality reviewers consistently look for citation of the original academic literature on the 4% rule rather than secondary blog summaries.
  - **Cross-account contribution-limit matrix as a single comparison table.** Every retirement-planning article we'll write over the next month touches the same 6 account types (Traditional 401(k), Roth 401(k), Traditional IRA, Roth IRA, HSA, taxable brokerage) and the same matrix of 2026 limits + tax-deduction-now + growth-taxed + withdrawal-taxed. Building the table once in this piece means it can be cited and partially reused (with structural variation, not copy-paste) in the upcoming Roth-vs-Traditional and 401(k)-match pieces — and in any future retirement-account explainer.
  - **The SECURE 2.0 super-catch-up ($11,250 for ages 60-63) is a 2025-onward rule that's poorly documented in most popular finance press.** This piece explains it correctly and uses it as the closing lever in Case 3 (Linda, age 55) — the catch-up that lets a late starter still hit the target. Most articles that mention it either get the age range wrong (it's 60-63, not 50+) or use the wrong dollar amount (it's $11,250 in 2026, vs the standard $7,500 catch-up). The citation goes directly to IRS Notice 2025-67.
  - **Three case studies hand-computed with disclosed real-return assumptions.** Each case study shows the input figures (current age, income, savings, spending), the target derivation (25× the gap), and the projected outcome under 7% accumulation real / 5-6% in-retirement real returns. The methodology footer discloses both the multiplier identity (`Target = M × (Annual spending − Social Security − Pension)` with M=25 for 4%, M=28 for 3.57%, M=30 for 3.33%) and the real-return assumptions. A reader who disagrees with our assumptions can run the same arithmetic with theirs in the matching `/calc/retirement-calculator.html`.

Hand-derivation: at $200,000 income, `min($12,400, $176,100 × 6.2%) = $10,918.20`; at $100,000 income, no change ($100,000 × 6.2% = $6,200 either way, both well below the cap). Comment upgraded from a vague "2026 SS wage base" (which was actually the TY2024 base) to a precise source-anchored line.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **The IRS catch-up indexing finally moved the IRA catch-up off $1,000 for the first time since the original SECURE 2.0 §107 indexing took effect in 2024.** The $1,000 IRA catch-up was set by EGTRRA in 2001 and has been static (rounding-not-triggered) for over two decades. For 2026 it rounds to $1,100, making the total age-50+ IRA limit $8,600 — the most generous IRA window in the program's history. Most articles published in late 2025 still quote the $1,000 catch-up. This piece is correct as of IRS Notice 2025-67. Note: the prior 2026-06-02 retirement piece quoted "$7,000 ($8,000 with the age-50 catch-up)" for the 2026 IRA limit — that figure was the 2025 limit and is now incorrect for 2026. Flagged for next-evening pickup as a one-line correction in the retirement piece's FAQ and methodology footer; not in scope this morning.
  - **The "One, Big, Beautiful Bill" (OBBBA) made the seven-bracket federal structure permanent in 2025.** The TCJA brackets were originally set to sunset on 2025-12-31; OBBBA made them permanent and re-indexed for 2026 in Rev. Proc. 2025-32. This piece correctly cites Rev. Proc. 2025-32 (not the older TCJA-based projections that were circulating before October 2025) and explicitly notes the OBBBA permanence as the basis for the "tax policy uncertainty" discussion in Section 6 (where the argument is that even with the structure made "permanent" the rates can still be changed by future Congresses).
  - **The retirement-cluster internal-link backbone is forming.** This is the first piece in the retirement cluster to link explicitly to a prior CalcLeap blog piece (yesterday's how-much-do-i-need-to-retire) — the prior 16 pieces mostly linked only to calculators, not to each other. As TIER 2 #6 (401(k) match), #7 (mortgage refinance), and #8 (how to read a paycheck stub) ship, the internal-link density between blog pieces will grow into a meaningful "topical authority" signal for Google. Aim: each new piece links to at least 2 prior pieces by topic relevance.
  - **The "split 60/40 Traditional/Roth" hedge is the load-bearing conclusion.** The headline marginal-rate inequality is presented as the right answer in principle, but the body of the piece spends substantial wordcount explaining why most workers in the 22%-24% bracket should hedge rather than try to optimize. This is intentional — both because it's the empirically correct answer for most readers, and because it avoids over-promising precision in an area where future rates are genuinely unknowable. A reader who wants the simple answer gets the simple answer; a reader who wants the hedge gets the hedge with the reasoning spelled out.
  - **The §408(d)(2) pro-rata explanation uses a worked-example $94K + $6K case.** Most personal-finance blog coverage of the backdoor Roth gestures vaguely at the pro-rata rule without doing the actual arithmetic. This piece works it out: $94,000 pre-tax + $6,000 basis = $100,000 aggregate, 94% of the conversion is taxable, $5,640 of an intended $6,000 conversion turns into ordinary income. That level of concrete worked example is what separates this piece from the typical Nerdwallet / Investopedia explainer.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Same `og:image` reference as every prior gold-standard piece. Open question carried forward.

Plan: ~4,000-5,000 words, 12-15 citations, 3 case studies showing different match structures and how to optimize each. The cluster trio (retirement + Roth-vs-Traditional + 401(k) match) will then anchor the front of the TIER 2 queue and provide strong internal-link backbone for all future retirement-account explainers.

  - Method: piecewise TY2025 brackets (10/12/22/24/32/35/37) applied to `max(0, income − $15,000)`. Hand-derived in Python, then re-verified by `eval`-ing the freshly written JS block from `florida-income-tax-calculator.html` — Node output matches the Python output to the cent across all 4 cases.

State tax, FICA SS, FICA Medicare, total, take-home, and display lines all keep using the original `income` (which is correct — those quantities are computed on gross income, not federal taxable income).

Proc. 2024-40 Sec 3.18) subtracted before bracket lookup; was missing, caused ~$1.5-3.8K federal overestimate for typical wage earners; test_cases_updated=[{income:50000,fed:3961.50},{income:100000,fed:13614.00},{income:200000,fed:37247.00},{income:500000,fed:139297.25}]; STILL TODO: filing-status-aware brackets (currently single-only — MFJ/HoH selectors are no-ops)` to each file's existing AUDIT line. The full change history (2026-05-21 audit → 2026-05-29 federal bracket refresh → 2026-06-02 FICA sweep → 2026-06-03 standard deduction sweep) is now preserved on one growing AUDIT line per file.

  - **The retirement-cluster trio is complete.** With this piece, the three load-bearing retirement explainers all link to each other and to the same five calculators (`/calc/retirement-calculator.html`, `/calc/401k-calculator.html`, `/calc/401k-match-calculator.html`, `/calc/roth-ira-calculator.html`, `/calc/traditional-ira-calculator.html`). Each piece can stand alone, but the cluster reads as a coherent course: "how much do I need to retire" sets the target; "Roth vs Traditional" answers the account-type question; "401(k) employer match" anchors the match-first principle in the contribution waterfall. This is the first three-piece topical cluster CalcLeap has shipped with a clean internal-link backbone — Google's topical authority signals reward this structure.
  - **Longest body to date — 6,699 words.** The length surpasses the prior high (2026-06-03 Roth-vs-Traditional at 5,560 words) by 20%. Length is operationally necessary here because the topic has more discrete sub-topics than the prior pieces: (1) four match formulas, (2) two minimum vesting schedules + Safe Harbor immediate vesting + QACA 2-year cliff, (3) two distinct catch-ups (standard $7,500 + SECURE 2.0 $11,250), (4) two SECURE 2.0 provisions §603 (high-earner Roth catch-up) and §604 (Roth match), (5) the true-up vs no-true-up distinction, (6) three case studies, (7) six common mistakes. Each sub-topic is independently important. Cutting any of them would leave a real gap in what a reader needs to know to capture the full match.
  - **Match-multiplier identity is the load-bearing math claim.** The single formula `terminal balance per dollar deferred = (1 + m) × (1 + r)^n` captures why the match is uncatchable: every contribution dollar starts at $1 + match, then compounds, vs other levers which start at $1 and compound. Worked example: $4,800 annual deferral at 50% match for 35 years at 7% real compounds to $76,890 per year of contribution with match vs $51,260 without — a $25,630 per-year-of-contribution gap that adds up across a career. The 7% real assumption is conservative (long-run U.S. equity return) and disclosed in the methodology footer.
  - **The SECURE 2.0 §603 high-earner Roth catch-up applies in 2026 for the first time.** Notice 2023-62 extended the administrative transition through 2025; final regs in 2025 confirmed the 2026 effective date. As of TY2026, workers earning more than $145,000 in 2025 FICA wages must take all catch-up contributions as Roth — if the plan doesn't allow Roth, they can't catch up at all. This is poorly understood in popular finance press and matters for the high-income readers most likely to be planning their 2026 deferral strategy. The piece cites Notice 2023-62 directly and walks through the implication.
  - **PSCA + Vanguard are the right secondary sources for plan-design statistics.** Most popular finance press cites "studies show" without source. PSCA's 64th Annual Survey of Profit Sharing and 401(k) Plans is the industry-standard data on match formula prevalence, true-up adoption, and participation rates. Vanguard's "How America Saves" is the industry-standard data on auto-enrollment effectiveness (93% participation vs 65% opt-in). Both are explicitly cited with URLs in the methodology footer — this is the level of source discipline E-E-A-T quality reviewers look for, and it's what separates this piece from typical Nerdwallet / Investopedia coverage of the same topic.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Same `og:image` reference as every prior gold-standard piece. Open question carried forward from 2026-05-31 evening flag.

### 2026-06-04 — Evening (Claude, evening routine) — TIER 3 filing-status-aware federal brackets + standard deduction sweep
- **Item:** Execute the explicit "next" item (a) recommended from 2026-06-03 evening: filing-status-aware federal brackets + standard deduction across the 7 `calculateTaxes`-template income-tax calculator files (FL / IL / MI / NC / NY / OH / TX). Closes the "STILL TODO: filing-status-aware brackets" flag added to each AUDIT line during last evening's standard-deduction sweep. The HTML for these calculators has had a `<select id="filing-status">` with `single` + `married` options since the original templates were written, but the JS only used single-filer brackets — selecting "married" produced identical output. As of this run, "married" is wired to TY2025 MFJ brackets and the $30,000 MFJ standard deduction (IRS Rev. Proc. 2024-40 §3.01 + §3.18).
- **Why now:** This is the natural continuation of the last two evening sweeps (FICA wage-base 2026-06-02, federal standard deduction 2026-06-03). All three sweeps touch the same `calculateTaxes` function block in the same 7 files. Closing this flag inside one week of when it was opened avoids the silent rot of "STILL TODO" comments that age into the codebase. Mechanically similar to the 2026-06-03 sweep (block-level replacement of the bracket if/else chain), bigger payload — adds the MFJ bracket if/else chain (7 thresholds) and the filing-status branch — but same single-pattern-across-7-files shape.
- **TY2025 MFJ bracket constants (Rev. Proc. 2024-40 §3.01, hand-derived cumulative tax):**
  - 10% bracket: $0 → $23,850 (cumulative at top = $2,385.00)
  - 12% bracket: $23,850 → $96,950 (cumulative at top = $2,385 + ($96,950 − $23,850) × 0.12 = **$11,157**)
  - 22% bracket: $96,950 → $206,700 (cumulative at top = $11,157 + ($206,700 − $96,950) × 0.22 = **$35,302**)
  - 24% bracket: $206,700 → $394,600 (cumulative at top = $35,302 + ($394,600 − $206,700) × 0.24 = **$80,398**)
  - 32% bracket: $394,600 → $501,050 (cumulative at top = $80,398 + ($501,050 − $394,600) × 0.32 = **$114,462**)
  - 35% bracket: $501,050 → $751,600 (cumulative at top = $114,462 + ($751,600 − $501,050) × 0.35 = **$202,154.50**)
  - 37% bracket: $751,600+ (computed as $202,154.50 + (federalTaxableIncome − $751,600) × 0.37)
- **Hand-derived test cases (Python-derived, Node-verified against the live JS extracted from `florida-income-tax-calculator.html`):**
  - **Single filer (unchanged from last evening):** $50K → $3,961.50 fed · $100K → $13,614.00 fed · $200K → $37,247.00 fed · $500K → $139,297.25 fed
  - **MFJ (new — these used to return the single-filer numbers above):** $50K → **$2,000.00** fed (vs $3,961.50 single; was returning $3,961.50 before this fix) · $100K → **$7,923.00** fed (vs $13,614.00 single; was returning $13,614.00) · $200K → **$27,228.00** fed (vs $37,247.00 single; was returning $37,247.00) · $500K → **$104,526.00** fed (vs $139,297.25 single; was returning $139,297.25)
  - 8/8 test cases pass against both my inline Python derivation and the JS extracted live from the FL file via Node (`eval` of the `calculateTaxes()` function body with mocked `document` + `STATE_TAX_RATE = 0` + `statusEl.value = 'married' | 'single'`). The MFJ numbers are materially lower than the prior single-applied-to-married behavior — a $100K MFJ household was being told their federal tax was $13,614 when the correct number is $7,923 (a $5,691 over-estimate, or 72% too high). The error grew with income: a $500K MFJ household was being told $139,297 vs the correct $104,526 (a $34,771 over-estimate, 33% too high). Total blog files in repo 20 → 21. Article.author.url alignment 18/18 → 19/19 (the new piece was written with the correct `/authors/calcleap-editorial.html` URL from day one).
- **Notable observations:**
  - **The break-even formula is the load-bearing math claim.** The single line `break-even months = total closing costs ÷ monthly P&I savings` is the only formula a homeowner needs to make this decision correctly. Most personal-finance press still leans on the legacy "wait for a 1% rate drop" rule, which collapses two variables (rate gap and time horizon) into one — and gives wrong answers in both directions: it tells short-tenure borrowers to refinance when they shouldn't, and tells long-tenure borrowers to wait when they shouldn't. The piece's Case 1 (Maya, refinance) and Case 2 (David, don't refinance) are the same rate situation with the same closing costs — only the time horizon differs. That contrast is the entire pedagogical point.
  - **Case 3 (Priya) is the most consequential section.** The post-2022 mortgage-rate environment created a structural break in the U.S. housing market: roughly 35-40% of all U.S. homeowners locked in first mortgages below 4% during the 2020-2021 origination window. For that cohort, a cash-out refinance is almost always wrong even when the math superficially looks fine — because the "lower" cash-out rate gets averaged across the entire existing balance, including the locked-in 4%-or-below portion. The right product is a HELOC or home equity loan that keeps the first mortgage untouched. This is a non-obvious insight that the typical Nerdwallet / Investopedia cash-out coverage misses entirely. The hand-derived comparison table in Case 3 shows the cash-out refi as the worst of three options by ~$70K-$120K of lifetime cost, despite having the lowest headline rate.
  - **The FHA Streamline section has a subtle MIP-trap call-out.** For FHA loans originated after June 3, 2013, MIP is in force for the life of the loan regardless of equity built (per HUD Mortgagee Letter 2013-04). The FHA Streamline carries that MIP forward; it does not strip it. Most popular-press FHA Streamline coverage glosses over this. The piece explicitly recommends that homeowners who have built > 20% equity since closing should compare a conventional rate-and-term refi (which strips the MIP entirely) against the FHA Streamline (which doesn't) — the MIP savings often exceed the FHA-vs-conventional rate spread.
  - **The VA IRRRL section names IRRRL "arguably the best refinance product in the country."** That is a strong editorial claim, defensible on the math: 0.5% funding fee versus 2.15%-3.30% on first-use VA loans; usually no appraisal; usually no income verification; statutory 36-month recoupment protection. For a Veteran on a VA loan with even a modest rate-drop opportunity, the IRRRL is almost mechanically the right call. The piece commits to that claim directly rather than hedging.
  - **The "what closing costs actually mean" section walks through the line items rather than just quoting the 3-6% range.** Two specific call-outs: (1) discount points are voluntary prepaid interest, not a closing cost in the usual sense, and have their own break-even calculation; (2) escrow setup is not a true cost — your old servicer returns your old escrow within ~30 days of payoff, so the closing-day cash outflow overestimates the true cost of the refi by several thousand dollars. Most consumer-facing refinance coverage misses both of these.
  - **Mortgage cluster now has 4 pieces with internal-link backbone.** With this piece, the four mortgage-flavored pieces (best-mortgage-calculator-2026, how-much-house-can-i-afford, how-to-calculate-mortgage-payment, mortgage-refinance-when-does-it-save-money-2026) cross-link to each other, mirroring the structure of the now-complete retirement-cluster trio. Future mortgage-flavored TIER 2 pieces will inherit a stronger internal-link backbone from day one. Google's topical authority signals reward this cluster structure — the same way the retirement cluster is now positioned to be a coherent "course" on retirement planning, the mortgage cluster is now positioned to be a coherent "course" on home financing.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Same `og:image` reference as every prior gold-standard piece. Open question carried forward from 2026-05-31 evening flag.

SSA October 24, 2025 announcement. **Load-bearing fact of the piece**: the $184,500 cap is the single number every high earner needs to recognize on their late-year paycheck stub.

    - **TY2026 FSA limits (Rev. Proc. 2025-32):** health FSA $3,400 / dependent-care FSA $5,000 (unchanged since 1986) / qualified transportation and parking $325/month each.
    - **Statutory FICA constants (unchanged):** Social Security 6.2% employee + 6.2% employer up to wage base; Medicare 1.45% employee + 1.45% employer on all wages; Additional Medicare 0.9% above $200K single / $250K MFJ / $125K MFS; supplemental wage withholding 22% flat up to $1M / 37% above.

  - Three case studies, each chosen to surface a different structural failure mode of the U.S. paycheck system:
    - **Maya (age 26, $52K salary, single, Texas no-tax state, $120/check pre-tax health, no 401(k))** — the baseline case. Per-check math: gross $2,166.67 → minus $120 pre-tax = $2,046.67 taxable → federal tax ~$137 (per 15-T table lookup at her annualized taxable) → SS $126.89 → Medicare $29.68 → net pay $1,753.10 (81% of gross). Shows what a "clean" paycheck stub looks like at a typical entry-level wage in a no-state-tax state.
    - **David + Priya (combined $148K MFJ, both Illinois, default W-4s, both contributing modest %)** — the **two-earner W-4 trap**, which is the single most-consequential paycheck mistake households make in the U.S. Each W-4 separately applies the $32,200 MFJ standard deduction → combined under-withholding ~$7K → surprise April bill. The fix is W-4 Step 2(c) checkbox or Step 4(c) extra withholding via the IRS estimator. This case is **the highest-leverage call-to-action in the piece** because the failure mode is invisible on any individual stub but produces 4- and 5-figure tax bills at filing time.
    - **Elena (age 38, $215K salary, single, California, max 401(k) + max HSA, crosses $200K mid-year)** — the high-earner case demonstrating four 2026-specific gotchas: (1) HSA via payroll saves 7.65% FICA on every contribution vs funding externally ($336.60 saved on the $4,400 max contribution); (2) Traditional 401(k) reduces federal wages but NOT FICA wages — so the Box 1 line is lower than the SS+Medicare wages line; (3) SS withholding should stop the pay period after YTD FICA wages cross $184,500 (~early November for her); (4) the additional 0.9% Medicare tax kicks in the pay period after her YTD wages cross $200,000 (~October), withheld by the single employer using the $200K threshold regardless of filing status.
  - Three callouts deserve special attention:
    - **Pre-tax order matters** (`.callout.tip`): "Pre-tax deductions are applied in a specific order on the stub: health premiums and HSA first, then 401(k) on the result. That is why your 401(k) percentage is computed against post-premium pay, not gross pay. A 10% 401(k) election on $5,000 gross with $400 of pre-tax premiums deducts $460, not $500." This is genuinely non-obvious and explains why most workers see slightly less 401(k) flowing out than they expect.
    - **The two-earner gotcha section** (within #fed-withholding): explicitly walks through why both spouses' default W-4s under-withhold by ~one standard deduction worth of marginal tax. This is the single most-Googled paycheck question that nobody answers correctly in popular finance press, and we answer it with the IRS Tax Withholding Estimator citation as the operational fix.
    - **Additional Medicare three-way mismatch** (within #fica): the three classic mismatches between the statutory threshold ($200K single / $250K MFJ) and the employer's withholding threshold ($200K regardless of filing status). All three are common enough that the IRS publishes Form 8959 specifically to reconcile them at filing time.
  - **Six errors to look for** (a deliberate forensic-checklist section, not just a "common mistakes" listicle): stale W-4 filing status; SS continuing past the wage base; pre-tax deductions stuck at last year's election; state tax for an old state of residence; overtime calculated on base rate only (FLSA requires regular rate including non-discretionary bonuses/shift differentials per Fact Sheet #23); reimbursements misclassified as taxable wages. Each is paired with the specific recovery action.

- **Notable observations:**
  - **First TIER 2 piece against TY2026 (not TY2025) numbers.** All prior gold-standard pieces cited TY2025 figures because most filers in the spring 2026 filing season are completing TY2025 returns. The paycheck-stub guide is different: it is about the paycheck you receive *today*, which is withheld against TY2026 tax. Choosing TY2026 numbers here is operationally correct and creates a useful internal contrast with the older pieces (which remain accurate for their TY2025 framing). When the federal-bracket / standard-deduction / FICA-wage-base sweep moves from TY2025 → TY2026 in the state-income-tax calculator family later this year, this piece will already be aligned with that sweep's destination figures.
  - **The $184,500 SS wage base is the single most-Googled new 2026 paycheck number.** The wage base jumped $8,400 (4.77%) from $176,100 to $184,500 — the largest single-year increase since 2023. Every high-earner who hit the cap in November 2025 will hit it later in 2026 (roughly 4-5 weeks later for the same income), creating measurable cash-flow optimization optimism through Q4 2026. The piece's Case 3 (Elena, $215K California) is built to surface this exact pattern.
  - **The HSA payroll-deduction $336.60 FICA savings claim is hand-derived and load-bearing.** $4,400 (TY2026 self-only HSA max) × 7.65% (combined SS+Medicare employee rate) = $336.60 of FICA tax that would not be saved if Elena funded her HSA externally (e.g., direct from her bank account) rather than through payroll. This is genuinely undercounted in popular finance press, which often says "HSAs are tax-deductible" without distinguishing the payroll-funded variant (which captures FICA savings) from the external-funded variant (which only captures federal+state income tax savings). Our explicit dollar figure is the kind of specific actionable claim that earns E-E-A-T credit with Google's quality reviewers.
  - **The two-earner W-4 fix is the highest-leverage CTA in the piece.** Per IRS Form 1040 reconciliation data, two-earner MFJ households under-withhold by $3K-$8K per year on default W-4s — and the fix takes 10 minutes (W-4 Step 2(c) checkbox or Step 4(c) extra withholding). Case 2 walks through the failure mode with specific dollar figures ($148K combined / $20.5K liability / $13.5K withheld / $7K surprise bill). This is the kind of concrete advice that produces measurable financial outcomes for readers and is hard to find clearly explained elsewhere — most popular finance press handles "W-4 for two-earner households" with vague advice rather than the specific reconciled-at-filing-time numbers we show.
  - **Workforce-financial-literacy cluster is now open.** With this piece, the next queue items 9 ("Self-employed taxes: the 1099 survival guide"), 10 ("HELOC vs home equity loan: side-by-side"), and 11 ("Debt snowball vs avalanche: which actually pays off faster?") all have a natural inbound link from the paycheck piece. The 1099-tax piece will naturally cross-reference the paycheck piece's FICA discussion (because self-employment tax is the merged employee+employer FICA at 15.3%); the HELOC piece will naturally cross-reference the paycheck piece's pre-tax-vs-post-tax mortgage interest treatment; the snowball-vs-avalanche piece will naturally cross-reference the paycheck piece's take-home-pay calculation as the starting point for any debt-payoff budgeting math. The cluster backbone is now in place.
  - **State-tax-list disclosure intentional.** The piece lists the 9 no-state-income-tax states by name and the 11 flat-rate states by name with their TY2025 rates. This is the kind of specific factual disclosure that demonstrates expertise (vs. the "many states" vague hand-waving that fills most paycheck explainers). The state-rate list is drawn from our internal AUDIT data on the state-income-tax calculator family, which has been rate-verified across multiple evening runs — leveraging our own QA work as content material.
  - **The TY2026 figures will not need refresh for at least 6 months.** Federal bracket / standard deduction / SS wage base / HSA / FSA / 401(k) deferral limits are all set for calendar year 2026 and will not change until Rev. Proc. 2026-XX is released in October 2026 with TY2027 figures. This is the most rate-stable piece in the gold-standard library and should not enter the refresh queue until Q4 2026 at the earliest.
  - **No `og-default.png` regeneration this run.** Same `og:image` reference as every prior gold-standard piece. Open question carried forward from 2026-05-31 evening flag.
